Grand Unified Theory

Urth list · 129 messages · 2010-08-19 to 2010-09-13

2010urthThe Book of the Long SunThe Book of the New SunThe Book of the Short SunMark LewinRyan DunnLee BermanMo Holkar / UKGJack SmithAntónio Pedro MarquesDavid StockhoffJames WynnDan'l Danehy-OakesJeff WilsonDave TallmanRoy C. Lackey
The thread: The thread began with Lee Berman's Grand Unified Theory that ancient gods and monsters were shapechanging non-human visitors who interbred with humans, applied first to The Book of the New Sun and then to the Long and Short Sun books. James Wynn built on it with clone and Hyacinth-Fava theories, which drew sharp challenges from Roy Lackey and Dave Tallman on chronology, evidence, and textual support. Parallel digressions on the pronunciation and etymology of Typhon and typhoon ran for days without resolution, and later the naming-by-animal-and-body-part theory faced mockery and cherry-picking charges that turned personal. The thread ended in impasse and drift rather than agreement.
  • Lee Berman: ancient gods were shapechanging alien visitors who interbred with humans, a grand unified theory across Wolfe's books
  • James Wynn: Hyacinth and Fava are the same character, and Silk is a clone of Fish
  • Roy C. Lackey: Hy died before Fava reached Blue, and the clone and naming claims go beyond the text
  • Dave Tallman: demands textual evidence for the Hyacinth-Fava equation and rejects the Rajan-as-Pike reading
  • David Stockhoff: argues typhoon came from a Cantonese or Indian source, and pushes a family-naming system
  • António Pedro Marques: linguists can reconstruct ancient pronunciations and typhoon is not from Typhon

Themes: Names and their meanings, Myth and folklore sources, Robots, androids and artificial life

2010-08-19 Lee Berman #

Proposes a unified theory of Wolfe's worldview: ancient gods and monsters were real non-human visitors, shapechangers who interbred with early humans, and this explains The Book of the New Sun and the megatherians on Blue and Urth. Grounds the theory in claimed Wolfe interviews, the Eden and Flood narratives, and a reading of the 5HC shapechanger remark.
proposes · author-and-biography · allegory-and-typology · theories: Ancient gods and monsters were alien shapechangers; Shapechanger DNA impurities explain human-alien interbreeding; Megatherians originated on Blue, not Urth

Well, I'll try to present my Grand Unified Theory or spill my GUT as it were. It isn't that profound or unique and I doubt it will serve anyone else as an epiphany. But perhaps it can be slightly helpful to someone.

In trying to figure out BotNS, and its accessories I eventually came to think that the mysteries might be too impenetrable on their own and it might be more productive to try to understand Gene Wolfe and how he works. I think I have read ever bio and interview available, most of them several times. I think his personal history is an important bit of information in understanding some of the things in his books. His current family situation is important for understanding certain things in his later books.

Anyway, what I was really looking for was not my own Grand Unified Theory but Gene Wolfe's. If I could understand how he viewed the world then his books might make more sense. He seems to be a person who has an amazingly broad knowledge in the physical sciences, anthropology, Western literature, mythology, religion and is a very religious person himself.

If he were to attempt to form his own GUT which would tie all his knowledge and beliefs of the world together what would it look like? From his interviews I think it is something like this:

Q. Why is there so much congruence and convergence in the themes of ancient religions and early Judeo-Christianity? Multiple gods with animalistic natures, giants, shapechangers, etc.

A. People in the olden days were just as smart and insightful as we are. Chances are they were all seeing something real which gave rise to the similarities in early religions. The gods, monsters and magic of the olden days was based on something people saw then which we are not seeing today.

Q. Why do we not see the same mythological inspirations that our ancestors saw?

A. They have been cleansed (though perhaps not fully) from the world by two Judeo-Christian events, Judeo= Flood. Christian= Savior.

The Garden of Eden seems to depict forest-dwelling human beings who don't use fire or tools or wear clothing. Anthropology tells us we have ancestors who fit that description. The Original Sin story seems to suggest that these simple human beings were brought out of their idyllic existence through the actions and temptations of non-human intelligence.

Trying to retain a scientific view, there were no other extant intelligent species on earth so the source of this non-human intelligence must be extra-terrestrial. Aliens. That's what we'd call them anyway. Back then they were called gods, angels, monsters, demons, fallen angels, giants, vampires nephilim, etc. etc.

Some were relatively good to the simple primates species they found on earth, introducing them to various forms of knowledge and technology. Others were bad, seeking to dominate and enslave us and even interbreed with us.

Interbreeding with humans is a common theme in mythology (even in the Bible) but it doesn't sound scientifically possible. But, a very common theme in ancient mythologies is that the gods/demons are shapechangers. If such a shape change was accompanied by a DNA change then interbreeding becomes possible- with the proviso, as noted in 5HoC, that a shapechanger would not be identical to its model as it would want to retain the ability to change shape. Thus the DNA of a shapechanging alien imitating a human would not be purely human but contain impurities.

I suppose there are more details, but that's basically it. I get the sense that this is Gene Wolfe's view of what happened in the ancient world. And I find that by keeping it in mind I can make sense of pretty much everything, including dreams and stories and plays, in BotNS. It helps with Long and Short Sun also and others also.

Thus I find the question of origin easy to answer when it is observed that there seems to be megatherian creatures on Blue and on Urth. Urth is a human planet, which has been invaded and corrupted by aliens. So megatherians originated on Blue and, most likely, other planets.

2010-08-22 James Wynn replying to Lee Berman #

Questions whether Lee Berman's Grand Unified Theory about understanding Wolfe's author rather than the mysteries can explain The Book of the Long Sun. Argues Silk may be both a clone of Typhon and the son of Typhon and the original Kypris, and asks why Typhon's daughter appears as a young girl in soul-form rather than more alien.
questions · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Silk is both a clone of Typhon and son of Typhon and Kypris

Lee,

Perhaps this works for you in The Book of the New Sun, but how do you incorporate it into The Book of the Long Sun?

Some see Silk as a clone of Typhon. Others think he is the son of Typhon and the original Kypris. I think both is true. But that would put a big question mark on the nature of Silk and Typhon's daughter as well. On that matter, when we see her in soul-form, she is a young girl. Shouldn't she have been, I don't know, a little more alien?

2010-08-22 Lee Berman replying to Lee Berman #

Asks James Wynn how Silk can be both a clone of Typhon and the child of Typhon and Kypris, and what Silk's daughter in soul-form and Hyacinth have to do with it. Offers his own view that humaniform Hierogrammates progress across universes until God is formed in the image of Man, and calls Long Sun less cosmologically important.
questions · puzzle-solving · theology-and-religion · theories: Hierogrammates progress toward humaniform form across universes; God eventually formed in the image of Man

James, I confess that understanding BotNS has always been my primary goal with other stories being more accessory to it. Urth/Earth is the home of humanity and it remains central as the creche of the race which is formed in the image of God. Weird-looking aliens are just not as important. If the humaniform Hierogrammates continue to progress in each successive universe as I tend to think, eventually it will also be that God has been formed in the image of Man. But perhaps that interpretation is heresy. Wouldn't be my first time.

Anyway my GUT finds Long and Short Sun stories much less cosmologically important. They are good for a continued elaboration on the Wolfean religious sequence that includes the need for both purification and salvation in the history of a planet (or Whorl as it were).

I need some clarification. I don't see how Silk can be an identical clone to Typhon but also be the genetically diverse product of sexual reproduction with Kypris. Also I'm afraid I need my memory jogged. Can you explain "Silk and Typhon's daughter" and her being a young girl in soul-form? Is Hyacinth involved with this in some way?

With some explanation of these, I hope to be able to answer your question.

2010-08-23 James Wynn replying to Lee Berman #

Proposes a Long Sun theory that Pas and Kypris match Typhon and Mamelta, Silk is the clone Fish, and Silk was implanted in Mamelta as a clone of Spring Wind, answering Lee Berman's question about Silk being both a clone and a sexually conceived child. Grounds the claim in a parrot named Chico in the character list, the Fish and Frog shepherd-wolf parallel, and the twin-son pattern of The Fifth Head of Cerberus.
proposes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Pas and Kypris equal Typhon and Mamelta; Silk is the clone Fish; Fish is a clone of Spring Wind implanted in Bird of the Woods

Premise 1: Pas & Kypris = Typhon & Mamelta = Spring Wind & Bird of the Woods (The character list in Book of the Long Sun includes "Chico", Mamelta's parrot. Mamelta never mentions a parrot and no parrot makes an appearance in the book, so I figure this is a clue from the author that Mamelta is "Bird of the Woods").

Premise 2: Silk is "Fish", who was raised by shepherds. (As opposed to Frog who was raised by wolves).
(Incidentally, the names Silk and Fish qualify the two as "brothers" at least as strongly as "Silk" and "Sand" and for the same reasons.)

Problem: The case for Silk being a clone of Typhon is, for me, as strong as Silk being the son of Typhon and the original Kypris.

Resolution: Fish was a clone of Spring Wind implanted in Bird of the Woods. It's a "Fifth Head of Cerberus" call back, where one is a clone and the other is the "natural son".

u+16b9

2010-08-23 James Wynn replying to Lee Berman #

Explains that the 'Silk and Typhon's daughter' in the question is Cilinia/Scylla, the young-girl soul-form Silk travels with at the end of Return to the Whorl. Grounds this in her claim to have named herself after the megatherian Scylla and in the myth of Scylla conspiring with Minos against her father Nisus, suggesting the two later merged.
answers · source-and-allusion

It was a typo. I meant Typhon's daughter, Cilinia/Scylla. At the end of "Return to the Whorl", Silk travels with Scylla-in-Oreb (essentially, the last remnant of Scylla). She is in the form of a young girl. She says she named herself Scylla after the megatherian with whom she was conspiring against her father (the "other" [ahem] Scylla conspired with King Minos against her city and her father, King Nisus.)

Then, big Scylla (apparently) absorbs little Scylla. So it is possible that the did eventually become one (as the mythical Scylla's undoubtably were as well).

James

2010-08-23 James Wynn replying to Lee Berman #

Proposes that Hyacinth of the Long Sun books is Fava of the Short Sun books, a theory the poster now holds with confidence after earlier suggesting Hyacinth was a male chem who transformed himself. Asks how Seawrack maps onto Hyacinth and whether the Love is their only common factor, after reading The Book of the Short Sun.
proposes · puzzle-solving · chronology-and-plot-logic · theories: Hyacinth is Fava; Hyacinth was a male chem who transformed herself

I'm the guy who once proposed (after just finishing "Long Sun") that maybe Hyacinth was a male chem who transformed herself into Hyacinth -- in some way I didn't understand, but analogous to Jonas becoming Miles.

After batting this around for a while, and having read "The Book of the Short Sun", I'm now quite confident that Hyacinth = Fava.

The question still out there is how does Seawrack map to Hyacinth? Is the Love the only common factor?

2010-08-23 Lee Berman replying to Lee Berman #

Lays out a sweeping "Grand Unified Theory" of The Book of the Long Sun and Short Sun in which shapeshifting alien parasites (Inhumi, Shadow Children) infiltrate humanity, creating gods and monsters, and Seawrack is a piece of The Mother. Responds to James Wynn's Hyacinth-Fava-Seawrack linkage and the Scylla backstory, linking love motifs to Wolfe's marriage and tying Typhon, Father Inire and Prometheus into the scheme.
proposes · myth-and-structure · allegory-and-typology · theories: Seawrack is a pinched-off portion of The Mother; Inhumi shapeshifting parasites seeded humanity and gods; Father Inire is an agent of Typhon; Horn's love for Nettle echoes Wolfe's marriage

As James said, I am writing to the list. I suspect the confusion is coming from the fact that James has used several posts to reply to different parts of my one earlier post.

I've little doubt that Horn's love for Nettle is an echo of Gene Wolfe's love for his wife, Rosemary. Likewise for the relationship between Horn and his sons.

I get the impression that there is a recognition of different kinds of loves in the story, some wholesome and plain, others passionate and...not so wholesome.

Silk's love for Hyacinth is clearly not very wholesome. Likewise Horn's love for Seawrack. (Typhon-Echidna-Kypris? a bit too nasty and god-like for me to unravel the morality)

I am convinced that Seawrack is a pinched off portion of The Mother. I do not understand how The Mother and Inhumi and Neighbors are related (surely somehow, yes?). But perhaps your perceived connection between Hyacinth-Fava-Seawrack can be helpful.

Thanks for the Scylla backstory! Yes, this does fall in line with my GUT. As tinkerbell Tzadkiel hopes to reuinte with big Tzadkiel, as (I think) Seawrack hopes to reunite with The Mother, so little, human Scylla hoped to reunite with big mama monster Scylla.

My GUT says that as humanity explores the galaxy, they encounter creatures (like Shadow Children and Inhumi) which have the ability to imitate, via shapeshifting. The imitation occurs down to the DNA level so there is sexual interbreeding possible. But the shapeshifting, asexual reproductive potential remains. These parasites are able to thus infect humanity among the stars and, of course eventually track humanity back to its source planet, Urth. Thus we get gods, monsters etc. I think exultants are tied to this- they are Urth's newest families and Thecla is wearing kraken jewelry. What might that symbolize?

If I understand correctly, Neighbors on Green were thoroughly infiltrated and corrupted by Inhumi, to the point of them going back to their home planet, Blue, and sowing war, infiltration and corruption there. Somehow (I guess) that led to the creation of a megatherian Mother on Blue.

A similar process happened on Urth. But Urth has guardian Angels looking after it as Blue does not. This is because Hierogrammates are derived from humans, not Neighbors. The Hierogrammates put the black hole in Urth's sun because it was being ruled by an alien monster in human form, named Typhon. Typhon says he learned that human beings follow their leader better if he has a recognizable human face. Later megatherian arrivals are, as Typhon recognizes, his rivals.

I think a legitimate debate continues on whether Father Inire might represent a version or agent of Typhon working against the new megatherian arrivals, as the Titan Prometheus was working against the Olympian usurpers by helping humanity. We have the Inire-Inuus-Pan-Pas-Typhon connection I think.

Does that make sense? (I realize it may not, to many)

2010-08-23 Roy C. Lackey replying to James Wynn #

Corrects James Wynn's claim that Mamelta's parrot Chiquito is a clue that Mamelta is the Bird of the Woods, noting the bird belonged to her parents on Urth. Supports the correction by quoting Mamelta's deathbed speech in Lake of the Long Sun, chapter 13, where she mentions Chiquito on his perch.
disputes · close-textual-reading · theories: Mamelta is the Bird of the Woods

The character list in LAKE reads:

"Chiquito, a parrot once owned by Mamelta's parents."

No, the word parrot is not mentioned in the book; neither does the bird make an appearance in it. It had belonged to Mamelta's parents, hundreds of years earlier back on Urth. She does mention the bird, shortly before her death. She believed that when she died she would get to go back home and be with her family.

"When I die, I'll go home. My mother and brothers . . . Chiquito on his perch in the patio." (LAKE, chapter 13, p-315)

-Roy

2010-08-24 James Wynn replying to Dave Tallman #

Argues that the surface contrasts between Hyacinth and Fava, the human crude fighter versus the witty inhuma storyteller, are deceptive, and that both characters are more dangerous and complicated than they appear. Supports this by citing Horn's remark that Hy could be sweet, the Rajan's warning about the inhumi's reptilian nature, and Silk's comment that Hy's fighting was a deception.
disputes · close-textual-reading

Everything you say here is 100% true. Absolutely true. Everything. Including that Hy is human and Fava is an inhuma.

However, don't be fooled by appearances. Horn said Hy *could* be crude. He also says she could be sweet and nice whenever she wanted to. The Rajan said that whatever their appearances, the inhumi retained their "reptilian nature". They were all dangerous opponents. Don't be fooled by Fava's appearance. Hy looked small and delicate too. And she was light as a feather. Silk said Hy's fighting ability was itself something of a deception.

The thing is its a big explanation. Bigger than Hy.

2010-08-24 Lee Berman replying to Lee Berman #

Argues that Spring Wind could be Typhon in a limited way, reading Typhon as an alien being linked to wind and dragons, while doubting James Wynn's Premise 2 about Silk and Fish sharing fraternity. Responds to Wynn's Grand Unified Theory premises, noting Silk's Urth connection is unclear and Spring Wind lacks Typhon's dragon traits and conquering nature.
disputes · source-and-allusion · puzzle-solving · theories: Spring Wind is Typhon; Pas and Kypris equal Typhon and Mamelta; Silk and Fish are brothers

I agree with Ryan. The stuff here doesn't make good pub or dinner table conversation.

I agree with Craig. Niggling at the shape of the individual jigsaw pieces is okay, but eventually I prefer to focus on the big picture taking shape. Thus the subject name of this thread.

I've been trying to consider James' premises from my own GUT perspective. I can't quite get to Premise 2. It isn't clear to me how the names Silk and Fish (and Sand) imply fraternity. Also the Fish and Frog story is said to take place on Urth (albeit an Urth in which wolves are able to talk and carry swords). I can't see how Silk was ever on Urth.

Premise 1 makes a bit more sense to me. Mamelta certainly does seem motherly in some ways. I can sorta like Spring Wind as Typhon for a couple reasons. I think Typhon, like the others with monster names on Urth, is an alien being. Spring Wind and Bird o' da Woods could be aliens as they are not on Urth but from "beyond the shores of Urth". Also, Typhon and his cognates, Typhon-Set, Pa(s)uzu and Vrita are mythological gods associated with wind, though it is a hot, searing desert wind causing drought, rather than a "Spring" wind. All have dragon associations also.

On the other hand, Spring Wind is quiet and obedient and with more interest in growing plants and stuff than conquest. A bit of a mama's boy. No dragon hints. Doesn't seem much like mythological or BotNS Typhon.

Either way I still appreciate James' efforts. He sees things I don't (and vice versa I assume). Though his GUT appears to be different than mine his views help me with mine.

2010-08-25 Dan'l Danehy-Oakes replying to Ryan Dunn #

Disputes the claim that Typhon's name in The Book of the New Sun has a single correct pronunciation, arguing that nobody can know how ancient Greek was spoken. Says he pronounced it TIE-fawn and questions whether psyche was SOO-kie, since only a text describing mouth movements could settle such points.
disputes · source-and-allusion

That seems to be a gimme.

Really? I pronounced it TIE-fawn.

I deeply mistrust anyone who claims to know how the ancients -- ANY ancients -- pronounced things. Unless the Greeks left a text (something like the opening movement of de Saussure's _Cours de Linguistique Generale_) that actually describes the mouth and throat movements involved in each letter, the most we have is a high probability that, say, the kappa was pronounced like the modern English kay. Was psyche pronounced SOO-kie? I don't know.

2010-08-25 James Wynn replying to Lee Berman #

Proposes that Silk is a clone of Fish, resting on a passage in Calde of the Long Sun where Silk muses that his name and Sand's are cognates, and on Kypris as Bird of the Woods. Also argues that Typhoon, Spring Wind and Typhon are linked and that Bill Ries is a cognate of Typhon, with figures like Charlemagne and Arthur cited as men made heroic by biographers.
proposes · close-textual-reading · source-and-allusion · theories: Silk is a clone of Fish; Silk reached Urth as the Rajan; Typhoon derives from Typhon in Wolfe; Bill Ries is a cognate of Typhon

What I am saying is this. We know Silk is a clone-with-improvements. I proffer that he is a clone of Fish.

From Calde of the Long Sun, Chap X:

"Silk did not hear Sand's reply; he was thinking about a great many things. Among them, names. His own and Sand's were similar-- each had four letters, each contained a single vowel, and each began with an S. They could not be related, however, because Sand was a chem and he a bio. Yet they were related by the similarity of their names. Not inconceivably (he found it a tantalizing idea). Sand was a cognate, a version of himself in some whorl of a higher order. Many things the Outsider had shown him seemed to imply that there were such whorls"

Look, I'm sure there are four-letter names in the Sun Cycle with as many similarities to "Silk" as "Fish" that have no possible connection. I can't think of one right now, but I'm sure they are there. But, I didn't get up one day and say "Hey! Silk and Fish both have four letters!". It was an after-thought upon deciding that Kypris was Bird of the Woods and Silk was probably her son in some way.

Silk *did* make it to Urth, sort of, in the form of the Rajan.

Spring Wind = Typhoon = Typhon
The word "typhoon" comes from "Typhon" -- not that it would prevent Wolfe from associating the two anyway if he wanted to.

Sev meets Typhon close up. Consequently, he doesn't come off very well. Pepin, Charlemagne, Alexander the Great, Cyrus, and King Arthur were probably all not very nice men if you got too close to them (there's a story about Arthur that is similar to King Herod's role in the birth of Jesus, and the nursery rhyme "When Good King Arthur Ruled This Land" has less laudatory versions). But they have all been catasterized as heroes by one biographer or another. They became bigger than themselves. I believe Bill Ries is a "cognate, a version" of Typhon "in some whorl of a higher order." The differing portrayals of Typhon and Spring Wind should be understood in the way that Cassie saw William Ries and Wallace Rusterman.

2010-08-25 James Wynn replying to António Pedro Marques #

Argues that Wolfe would happily link Typhon and typhoon even without a true etymological connection, since vowel shifts in language allow room for mischief. Responds to Antonio Pedro Marques's claim that the English word typhoon does not derive from Greek Typhon, conceding that linguistic change makes the question open.
disputes · source-and-allusion

Well, I'm not a linguist so I can't begin to break it down. But each language has it's own special tendencies to change over time. And the vowels change the fastest. The most common etymology I have read is that "typhon" came to Hindi through Parsi in order to become "typhoon". So there's a lot of room for mischief in there. But I'm sure you are right that there are alternate assertions by people who know a lot more about it than me. Still, the spellings and meanings are similar enough that I have no doubt Wolfe would happily associate them even if he knew for a fact that the words had no real etymological connection.

2010-08-25 David Stockhoff replying to James Wynn #

Argues that the word "typhoon" reached Cantonese from India as a nautical term for strong ocean storm winds, and that Greek colonizing of Central Asia gave it a plausible route. Responds to James Wynn's remarks on vowel change and the Hindi-Parsi etymology, noting that Webster's Third confirms the Greek origin while also mentioning Arabic.
disputes · source-and-allusion

Webster's Third implicates Arabic as well, but confirms the Greek connection.

The Greeks colonized and ruled Central Asia for hundreds of years. They would have had plenty of opportunity to come up with imaginative names for winds.

It must have gotten into Cantonese from India as a sort of technical nautical term for a strong ocean storm wind.

2010-08-25 Dan'l Danehy-Oakes replying to António Marques #

Admits he is no etymology expert and says he never meant to enter the derivation dispute, then questions whether ancient pronunciation of Latin and Greek can truly be known. Doubts modern reconstructions of Koine and Latin sounds, citing English dialect variation and the absence of recordings, and ties this to his earlier skepticism about grand unified theories. No Wolfe content.
questions

Antonio,

I bow to your expertise in how words derive from one another; though I find etymology endlessly fascinating I am far from expert in it. I never meant to enter the argument about what was derived from which.

As regards pronunciation, though, for all I know the ancient Romans may (though VERY improbably) have pronounced "crupta" as we pronounce "blanko."

Given how much variation takes place in just the English of England, and how much it shifts in a single lifetime, I find it hard to conceive that we know how anything was pronounced two millenia before the invention of recording technology. That Lat. "crupta" was very similar to Gk. "krypth" I have no doubt, or that (say) Gk Beta was more-or-less identical to Latin B. But how that B/Beta was pronounced? Probably not too far from a modern "B", but I would be utterly unsurprised to learn that it was, say, unvoiced, like a modern P. Can we know how, say, koine was pronounced, or do we merely build edifices of more-or-less probable speculations?

This may not be unrelated to my comment on grand unified theories earlier this week, if only through my own innate skepticism.

2010-08-25 António Pedro Marques replying to James Wynn #

Disputes the claim that "typhoon" derives from "Typhon," arguing the English word comes from a non-Greek language of the Indian Ocean margins. Supports the case with phonetic reasoning: Greek Typhon is pronounced differently, and English [ai] stems from the Great Vowel Shift plus a misread Greek y, making the link unlikely.
disputes · source-and-allusion

James Wynn wrote (25-08-2010 15:41):

That's actually open to question.
- _Typhon_ is an english spelling pronunciation of greek <tu'fwn>, which would be more or less 'two FAWN' [tu'fO:n] if read 'correctly'. - _typhoon_ is an english transcription of [tai'fun], from some language spoken along the margins of the Indic ocean.

Even if one posits that [tai'fun] is ultimately from the greek, one still would have to explain how did greek [tu] become [tai]. The english [ai] is merely from the english great vowel shift compounded with a misreading of greek <y> as [i], which it never was. Had english gotten the word by natural borrowing, rather than in misread written form centuries after, it wouldn't have [ai].

Not impossible, but unlikely.

Whole message, with quotes · original
James Wynn wrote (25-08-2010 15:41):

> The word "typhoon" comes from "Typhon" -- not that it would prevent
> Wolfe from associating the two anyway if he wanted to.

That's actually open to question.
- _Typhon_ is an english spelling pronunciation of greek <tu'fwn>, which 
would be more or less 'two FAWN' [tu'fO:n] if read 'correctly'.
- _typhoon_ is an english transcription of [tai'fun], from some language 
spoken along the margins of the Indic ocean.

Even if one posits that [tai'fun] is ultimately from the greek, one still 
would have to explain how did greek [tu] become [tai]. The english [ai] is 
merely from the english great vowel shift compounded with a misreading of 
greek <y> as [i], which it never was. Had english gotten the word by natural 
borrowing, rather than in misread written form centuries after, it wouldn't 
have [ai].

Not impossible, but unlikely.

2010-08-25 David Stockhoff replying to António Marques #

Proposes that the English word 'typhoon' arose by accident when an Italian, hearing a Chinese word under the influence of Greek 'typhon', translated it into English. Responds to António Marques's quotation of a dictionary entry tracing typhoon to Greek typhon, and calls the supposed Cantonese origin pure Orientalism.
proposes · source-and-allusion · theories: Typhoon derives from Greek typhon via mishearing Chinese

Is it possible that the supposedly Cantonese word "typhoon" is strictly an accident arising from a translation to English of a word an Italian heard in China under the influence of "typhon"?

That would make the word almost purely Western, gaining its Cantonese meaning only because a Chinese word sounds like it. Pure Orientalism.

Whole message, with quotes · original

an accident arising from a translation to English of a word an Italian 
heard in China under the influence of "typhon"?

That would make the word almost purely Western, gaining its Cantonese 
meaning only because a Chinese word sounds like it. Pure Orientalism.

> the modern word represents a coincidence and convergence of at least 
> two unrelated words of similar sound and sense. Tiphon  "violent 
> storm, whirlwind, tornado" is recorded from 1555, from Gk. typhon  
> "whirlwind," personified as a giant, father of the winds, perhaps from 
> typhein  "to smoke." The meaning "cyclone, violent hurricane of India 
> or the China Seas" (1588) is first recorded in T. Hickock's

> C?sar Frederick, a merchant of Venice, probably borrowed from, or 
> infl. by, Chinese (Cantonese) tai fung  "a great wind," from tu  "big"

2010-08-25 António Pedro Marques replying to Ryan Dunn #

Corrects Ryan Dunn's gloss on how the name Typhon should be pronounced, giving the Greek phonetics: a long vowel, stress by pitch rather than English stress, and an aspirated p that later became f. Responds to Dunn's earlier claim that the name is borrowed from Greek myth, and notes that Typhoon is a separate word.
disputes · close-textual-reading · source-and-allusion

Ryan Dunn wrote (25-08-2010 17:16):

Yes.

In 'proper' transcription ['tajf at n], with english words 'TIE fun'.

In greek, something like [tu'fO:n], 'too FAWN'
- the : is to indicate length
- the [u] for y would vary according to dialect, and would be like the french u or german ? in a certain classical pronunciation - the stress in greek wasn't like in english, rather spoken in a higher pitch - the ph was really an aspirated p, though it soon evolved into f

'Typhoon' is a different case.

Whole message, with quotes · original
Ryan Dunn wrote (25-08-2010 17:16):

> 1. Typhon from BotNS is a name borrowed from Typhon of Greek myth, king
> of all monsters?

Yes.

> 2. Most modern readers will instinctively pronounce his name: *TAY-fun*

In 'proper' transcription ['tajf at n], with english words 'TIE fun'.

> 3. The proper pronunciation, however, is: *TOO-fahn*

In greek, something like [tu'fO:n], 'too FAWN'
- the : is to indicate length
- the [u] for y would vary according to dialect, and would be like the 
french u or german ? in a certain classical pronunciation
- the stress in greek wasn't like in english, rather spoken in a higher pitch
- the ph was really an aspirated p, though it soon evolved into f


'Typhoon' is a different case.

2010-08-25 António Pedro Marques replying to Ryan Dunn #

Argues that Dan'l's awkward stress on a first syllable before 'fawn' comes from a vowel that is shortened but not fully reduced, since English post-stress vowels are sometimes only partly reduced. Supports the point with the example of 'corridor' not pronounced 'corrider' and notes that -on and -or names seem especially prone to this exception.
supports · theories: Post-stress vowels only partly reduced in English

Ryan Dunn wrote (25-08-2010 17:31):

I suspect Dan'l means his fawn vowel but shorter.

Post-stress vowels tend to be reduced in english, but not always completely. For instance, I think most people don't pronounce 'corridor' as 'corrider'. -on and -or names seem to be prone to this exceptionality.

2010-08-25 António Pedro Marques replying to Dan'l Danehy-Oakes #

Argues that the Greek pronunciation of Typhon is irrelevant to English 'typhoon', which instead traces to Cantonese tai fung or Arabic tufan, with the Greek link a later folk etymology. Supports the argument by explaining Greek y-to-u borrowing into Latin and quoting the Online Etymology Dictionary's account of the word's sources.
disputes · source-and-allusion · close-textual-reading · theories: English typhoon derives from Cantonese tai fung; Typhon in English reflects spelling pronunciation

But of course such works do exist, even if not using modern terminology and not always painting a complete picture. And much can be gathered from the subsequent evolution and loawords in other languages. F.i. greek <y> is borrowed into popular latin as <u> (krypth > crupta)*.

(*) Their descendents in english being crypt and grotto, the first borrowed from greek by people who could make sense of greek texts, the second from italian by people who could speak at least some italian.

But I and a lot of others do: not in any relevant dialect. Rather psoo-GHAY, to use the notation we've all come to loathe.

Nobody can tell exactly how it was pronounced, if nothing else because pronunciations varied according to region and epoch, as in any other language. But the issue here is not how T U Ph W~ N was pronounced, it's how it couldn't be. And there is no known dialect in which U could be [ai] as it is in english. And if it wasn't [ai], it's hard to see how it could have become [ai] in the asiatic language english took typhoon from. NB it did become [ai] in english typhon, but only because it was a spelling pronunciation based on a mistaken value for greek <?>. I haven't seen anyone propose such an unnatural route for typhoon.

The best I've seen so far is below, though it gives no evidence at all for the greek connection:

http://dictionary.reference.com/browse/typhoon
typhoon
the modern word represents a coincidence and convergence of at least two unrelated words of similar sound and sense. Tiphon "violent storm, whirlwind, tornado" is recorded from 1555, from Gk. typhon "whirlwind," personified as a giant, father of the winds, perhaps from typhein "to smoke." The meaning "cyclone, violent hurricane of India or the China Seas" (1588) is first recorded in T. Hickock's translation of an account in Italian of a voyage to the East Indies by C?sar Frederick, a merchant of Venice, probably borrowed from, or infl. by, Chinese (Cantonese) tai fung "a great wind," from tu "big" + feng "wind;" name given to violent cyclonic storms in the China seas. A third possibility is tufan, a word in Arabic, Persian and Hindi meaning "big cyclonic storm" (and the source of Port. tufao ), which may be from Gk. typhon but commonly is said to be a noun of action from Arabic tafa "to turn round." Online Etymology Dictionary, ? 2010 Douglas Harper

If you ask me, the probability is for arabic tufan for the portuguese version and cantonese tai fung for the english one. The english one having been folk etymologised as coming from greek tufwn, which in latin is typhon, thus getting its <y>.

2010-08-25 António Pedro Marques replying to Dan'l Danehy-Oakes #

Argues that linguists can reasonably reconstruct ancient pronunciations, since language change follows regular patterns observable in modern and historical evidence. Replies to Dan'l Danehy-Oakes's doubts about how Latin or koine was spoken, citing sound-based writing systems like Greek's and noting English's irregular spelling as a poor guide.
disputes

Dan'l Danehy-Oakes wrote (25-08-2010 21:48):

You're quite right to be skeptic in these matters, and it is true that what people who deal with history today do is to build such edifices. But there is a reason why linguists are confident, and that is the coherence of linguistic processes. Language change, as observed scientifically during the last couple of centuries, isn't random. It follows patterns ammenable to rational analysis and actually exhibits a huge degree of regularity. Now if one looks to what is attested in the last millennia, exactly the same processes appear to be taking place. It stands to reason that a knowledge of the modern forms and corroborating evidence from historical documents (either, as you asked for, the rare but existing descriptions of sounds by contemporary writers, or the way other languages borrow words, or even sometimes the way languages are transcribed in different alphabets than their own) is a solid basis to derive guesses that are so convincing as to become the default hypothesis. It's very, very far from looking at the letters they used and ascribing to them a pronunciation similar to what we use today for those letters or ones similar to them. In fact modern pronunciations, when resulting from a historic development, are seldom equal to the hypothesized original, though they can shed light on it.

Sound-based writing systems such as greek's present an advantage: they are a reflection (seldom perfect, though usually one can see where the defects are) of the language's phonemical structure, unconsciously derived by native speakers. Having their phonemical structure available is a bliss in terms of understanding how it may have evolved and even what kind of regional variation one can reasonably expect - because we can observe how modern languages behave in that regard, what types of sounds are likely to be present in a given system (e.g. if there are only N vowels, the most probable hypothesis is that those don't include some given ones), and what type of different pronunciations may be expected to appear without a break in the spelling tradition. Of course, all (or most) conjecture, but there comes a point when conjecture is so much in agreement with everything else that it takes very good reasons to doubt it.

English is perhaps a misleading example, for a variety of reasons. It looks alphabetical, but in fact it's full of homographs that are not homophones and the reverse. This is a historical artifact, and a rare one at that. Where old languages may have been subject to the same phenomenon, it was certainly to a much smaller extent, if any. Spelling usually *is* a guide to pronunciation, the more so in languages which didn't carry a burden of millennia of established orthographic subsystems from a handful of other languages (as english does).
Also, english is almost all diphthongs, which though legitimate isn't all that common, language-wise. You hardly have a 'pure' vowel in english, and when you multiply that across dialects you end up with a very unusual situation indeed.

Whole message, with quotes · original

> "blanko."
>
> Given how much variation takes place in just the English of England, and
> how much it shifts in a single lifetime, I find it hard to conceive that
> we know how anything was pronounced two millenia before the invention of
> recording technology. (...) Can we know how, say, koine was pronounced,
> or do we merely build edifices of more-or-less probable speculations?

2010-08-27 Dan'l Danehy-Oakes replying to Lee Berman #

Proposes that angels and devils are the same beings, distinguished only by obedience or rebellion toward God, and that pagan gods may be guiding worship away from worse beings. Responds to Lee Berman's question about Father Inire and the Cumaean by admitting there is no way to know whether they obey God, while noting they do not demand worship.
proposes · theology-and-religion · theories: Angels and devils are the same beings in different obedience; Pagan gods guide worship away from devils

\> For me it is a small intuitive leap to further assume that the angels and

I think angels would be the same beings when they are in obedience to God, while devils would be the same beings when in rebellion. The pagan gods are an interesting question - they are receiving the worship due only to God, but perhaps they are being used to guide worship away from worse beings (i.e., devils).

Again, the question is whether they are in obedience to God (or to the representatives of God they are given -- viz. Gabriel's comment) or not. We have no real way of knowing, that I can see, whether Inire and the Cumaean are or not. Certainly, though, they do not seem to demand worship, which would be a point in their favor.

2010-08-27 David Stockhoff replying to Dan'l Danehy-Oakes #

Argues that Severian's maxim "What is not said is important. But what is said is more important" should guide readings of Father Inire, whose gaps Wolfe would not leave unexplained if he were major. Responds to Dan'l Danehy-Oakes's view that angels and devils are the same beings in obedience or rebellion, using Dave Tallman's reading of Seven American Nights as a model of inference from positive evidence.
proposes · close-textual-reading · theology-and-religion · theories: Inire is a minor mystery because Wolfe would have said more

That reminds me of something Severian says when he is in the mountains. He muses on various topics relating to magic and says "What is not said is important. But what is said is more important."

This could be a tenet of Wolfeanism and a useful principle to apply to his fiction. We know that what his narrators do not tell us is important, but we should recall that what they tell us is more important. For example, Dave Tallman's interpretation of 7AN fills in lots of blanks, but also relies on positive information, such as (1) the forgery machine and (2) the presumptive forgery itself.

I wonder if this can help us figure out what to do with Inire. Yes, he is a gap, a void, a vacuum, a cipher, and therefore a mystery. But is he a very important one? Surely Wolfe would have told us a little more if he was.

2010-08-27 Lee Berman replying to Lee Berman #

Proposes a "Grand Unified Theory" that pagan gods and biblical angels and demons are the same kind of beings in Wolfe's religious outlook, with different names and interpretations. Applies this to The Book of the New Sun, arguing Father Inire and The Cumaean are analogues of pagan gods or fallen angels, not of angels, judged by their unmasked shortness and age rather than by beauty; it builds on Wolfe's quoted remark about real pagan powers.
proposes · theology-and-religion · allegory-and-typology · theories: Pagan gods and fallen angels are the same beings; Father Inire and The Cumaean are pagan gods or fallen angels

Mark Lewin-

Yes, thanks Mark. There are other Wolfe quotes which as clearly and unequivocally say the same thing about Gene Wolfe's personal religious philosophy. This is the most basic tenet of my Grand Unified Theory.

For me it is a small intuitive leap to further assume that the angels and demons of the Bible fit into a similar explanatory framework as gods and monsters of pagan mythology for Gene Wolfe. Same story, same "beings", different names and interpretations of their behavior.

Thus in BotNS there are some aliens who are noble and beautiful and others who are.. well, not so much.

I think it is easy to accept that, hey, Tzadkiel and Famulimus are good, Abaia and Erebus are bad.

But what about Father Inire and The Cumaean? How are we to judge their behavior? Does their hooded, cowled, old appearance mean anything? Are they more Yoda or Emperor Palpatine?

Hierodules wear masks to cover their youth and beauty while their height remains apparent. I get the impression that Father Inire's and The Cumaean's shortness and oldness is not a disguse. So, I must guess they are not analogues to angelic beings but are rather analogues to pagan gods or fallen angels or perhaps both. Pagan gods and fallen angels might be exactly the same thing in Gene Wolfe's philosophy.

(I think the issue is clouded by the fact that many of the fallen angels of Judeo-Christian belief can take a form which is beautiful to humanity. But I think that form is always described as beautiful and seductive, appealing to our base instincts rather than beautiful and noble appealing to our higher, divine nature. This is only fair- God has to give us SOME means of determining which is the right choice when faced with good and evil alternatives)

Mark Lewin-

Yes, thanks Mark. There are other Wolfe quotes which as clearly and unequivocally 
say the same thing about Gene Wolfe's personal religious philosophy. This is the 
most basic tenet of my Grand Unified Theory.
 
For me it is a small intuitive leap to further assume that the angels and 
demons of the Bible fit into a similar explanatory framework as gods and monsters 
of pagan mythology for Gene Wolfe. Same story, same "beings", different names and 
interpretations of their behavior.
 
Thus in BotNS there are some aliens who are noble and beautiful and others who are..
well, not so much.
 
I think it is easy to accept that, hey, Tzadkiel and Famulimus are good, Abaia and Erebus 
are bad.
 
But what about Father Inire and The Cumaean? How are we to judge their behavior? Does their
hooded, cowled, old appearance mean anything? Are they more Yoda or Emperor Palpatine?
 
Hierodules wear masks to cover their youth and beauty while their height remains apparent. I 
get the impression that Father Inire's and The Cumaean's shortness and oldness is not a 
disguse. So, I must guess they are not analogues to angelic beings but are rather analogues to
pagan gods or fallen angels or perhaps both.  Pagan gods and fallen angels might be exactly

2010-08-27 Mark Lewin replying to James Wynn #

Argues that Laura in There Are Doors is a surviving pagan goddess, supporting an earlier grand unified theory by pointing to Wolfe's own interview statement. Quotes Wolfe in the Jordan interview saying pagan gods were real powers that should not be worshipped, and that Laura was his attempt to imagine Aphrodite surviving into the modern world.
supports · author-and-biography · theology-and-religion · theories: Laura is a surviving pagan goddess such as Aphrodite

Apologies if anyone has made this connection already, but there's a comment by Wolfe in the Jordan
interview<http://mysite.verizon.net/~vze2tmhh/gwjbj2.html#fem> which would appear to back this up:

*JJ:* I want to ask you about Laura in *There Are Doors*. Having only been through it once and scanned it yesterday, I'm still not exactly sure about what Laura is.

*GW:* Laura is my idea of what a pagan goddess might be who survived into the Christian world. One of the places where I probably split off from conventional Catholic thinking is that I* believe that the gods of paganism were real*. I don't think that they are entitled to the worship that they received from the pagans. I think what many of the biblical writers are saying is, "Yes, these are real powers, but it is wrong for you to give to them the honors that are due to God alone." And I think that that is exactly correct. Now, if Aphrodite were to survive into the contemporary world, what would she be like? Well, Laura was a shot at trying to show what she might be like
Best regards,

Mark Lewin
[email]

>> Q. Why is there so much congruence and convergence in the themes of
>> ancient religions and
>> early Judeo-Christianity? Multiple gods with animalistic natures, giants,
>> shapechangers, etc.
>>
>> A. People in the olden days were just as smart and insightful as we are.
>> Chances are they
>> were all seeing something real which gave rise to the similarities in
>> early religions. The
>> gods, monsters and magic of the olden days was based on something people
>> saw then which we
>> are not seeing today.
>>
>> Q. Why do we not see the same mythological inspirations that our ancestors
>> saw?
>>
>> A. They have been cleansed (though perhaps not fully) from the world by
>> two Judeo-Christian
>> events, Judeo= Flood.  Christian= Savior.
>>
>> The Garden of Eden seems to depict forest-dwelling human beings who don't
>> use fire or tools or
>> wear clothing. Anthropology tells us we have ancestors who fit that
>> description. The Original
>> Sin story seems to suggest that these simple human beings were brought out
>> of their idyllic
>> existence through the actions and temptations of non-human intelligence.
>>
>> Trying to retain a  scientific view, there were no other extant
>> intelligent species on earth
>> so the source of this non-human intelligence must be extra-terrestrial.
>> Aliens. That's what we'd
>> call them anyway. Back then they were called gods, angels, monsters,
>> demons, fallen angels,
>> giants, vampires nephilim, etc. etc.
>>
>> Some were relatively good to the simple primates species they found on
>> earth, introducing them to
>> various forms of knowledge and technology. Others were bad, seeking to
>> dominate and enslave us
>> and even interbreed with us.
>>
>> Interbreeding with humans is a common theme in mythology (even in the
>> Bible) but it doesn't sound
>> scientifically possible. But, a very common theme in ancient mythologies
>> is that the gods/demons
>> are  shapechangers. If such a shape change was accompanied by a DNA change
>> then interbreeding
>> becomes possible- with the proviso, as noted in 5HoC, that a shapechanger
>> would not be identical to
>> its model as it would want to retain the ability to change shape. Thus the
>> DNA of a shapechanging
>> alien imitating a human would not be purely human but contain impurities.
>>
>> I suppose there are more details, but that's basically it. I get the sense
>> that this is Gene Wolfe's
>> view of what happened in the ancient world. And I find that by keeping it
>> in mind I can make sense
>> of pretty much everything, including dreams and stories and plays, in
>> BotNS. It helps with Long and
>> Short Sun also and others also.
>>
>>

2010-08-27 Lee Berman replying to Lee Berman #

Proposes that Father Inire and the Cumaean, as the oldest beings in the Commonwealth, are the hidden architects of its rigid hierarchy, war and repression, and that the odd extreme beautification in the books reflects their hand. Grounds this in their absence from UotNS, the Commonwealth's social shape, Jolenta, khaibits, Thecla's belladonna eyes and Merryn's facial skin, and an Azazel comparison.
proposes · puzzle-solving · myth-and-structure · theories: Inire and the Cumaean are the architects of the Commonwealth; Commonwealth beautification reflects Inire's hidden activity; Inire linked to Faunus and Inuus

Perhaps this is the intended point..that Wolfe is not sure how to judge Zeus and Apollo and Osiris and Vishnu etc. within a Christian framework. Perhaps the Whorl was a way to better explore this issue. What is the relationship between the (false) gods Pas and Scylla and Tartaros, etc. and The Outsider? The falsies serve their purpose, as all God's creations do, but are we right or wrong to worship such gods when we are not given Christian alternatives? Are they, as Dan'l implies, better than nothing?

Nonetheless, I think both do have followers. Explicitly we are told they have witches and soldiers and spies following them. And I think they play a much greater role in the Commonwealth than just that. Contrast them with Hierodules who take pains to not directly interfere with humanity.

Inire as not-very-important. Hmm, interesting to contemplate. A minority view I think even among the most skeptical..perhaps I'll counter with:

When it comes to Father Inire and The Cumaean it seems to be a very important point that they are the oldest beings in Commonwealth. They don't (openly) appear in UotNS (except for a goodbye hug from Inire before Severian's trip). So it might seem they don't exist at Apu Punchau's time nor during Typhon's rule nor Valeria's reign (and certainly not in Ushas).

Perhaps it is too speculative but I take their great age within a narrow timeframe to consider them (especially Inire) as the architects of the Commonwealth. Thus, we get a view of their greater activities simply by observing the shape and functioning of the Commonwealth itself.

In the Commonwealth I see a rigid social hierarchy with a lot of war and repression and torture and witchcraft, with a lot of defeatist philosophy on why it is the best possible society. One odd aspect that always puzzled me was the mysterious hints at extreme beautification. The mystery is most explicitly exposed with Jolenta, of course but we have khaibits and Thecla's belladonna eyes and Merryn's parchment stretched facial skin and a few other things.

Recently reading a description of fallen angel Azazel, cognate to Prometheus, gave me a slight "aha" moment.

(perhaps Faunus-Inuus-Inire invoked here)

2010-08-28 James Wynn replying to Dave Tallman #

Admits he cannot explain the coach dream in Return to the Whorl where Hyacinth and Orpine merge, but ties it to Silk's dream at the start of Lake of the Long Sun. Argues that Fava is still Hyacinth regardless of the dream's meaning, since many other clues point that way.
answers · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Fava is Hyacinth; Hyacinth becomes Orpine in dreams

They are not just dreams, but, no, I don't have an answer for this one, because it is tied up in the significance of Orpine -- who is a key centerpiece in Silk's enlightenment. And I don't understand Orpine. I'm pretty certain Hyacinth and Mora are not to be *directly* identified (in the way I am saying Fava and Hy are). But Orpine as connected to Hyacinth has always...well, not made *sense*...but she and Hyacinth are the two women in caskets that Silk stands over. And there is a knife at hand as Silk stands over Hy's coffin. So I see that there is (likely) an intended referred connection. In a way, Hy *does* become Orpine. And Silk stands over Fava's corpse too.

WHAT I DO UNDERSTAND (or think I do) is that this dream is connected to the dream Silk has at the beginning of "Lake of the Long Sun." In that one, Orpine has Mucor's face and she is "clawing" at the dagger in her side. And there too, Hyacinth sits beside him in the deathcoach. And, voila, Silk gives his old, bloodstained handkerchief --the one that covered Orpine's face --- to Hyacinth "to cover her nose and mouth". So here we again have a connection made between Hy and Orpine. But I don't claim to know what it means.

I'm not denying the significance of these dreams. But, I believe that, whatever the true meaning we ultimately come to understand, Fava will still be Hyacinth. There are just too many other expected actors and pointers. Fava being Hyacinth doesn't answer every open important mystery. It doesn't explain Mother or the connection between Seawrack and Hyacinth either.

2010-08-28 Dave Tallman replying to Lee Berman #

Argues that the dream Silk has at the start of Lake of the Long Sun links Orpine, Mucor and Hyacinth, and that the handkerchief gesture foretells Hyacinth's death before Silk reaches Blue. Grounds this in the dream's details (Orpine wearing Mucor's face, the bloodstained cloth, the stars at the grave) and in a passage on p. 158 of Return to the Whorl that recalls the dream.
proposes · close-textual-reading · myth-and-structure · theories: Orpine's face is Mucor's astral projection; Silk's dream foretells Hyacinth's death before Blue

the dream Silk has at the beginning of "Lake of the Long Sun." In that

one, Orpine has Mucor's face and she is "clawing" at the dagger in her

side. And there too, Hyacinth sits beside him in the deathcoach. And,

voila, Silk gives his old, bloodstained handkerchief --the one that

covered Orpine's face --- to Hyacinth "to cover her nose and mouth". So

here we again have a connection made between Hy and Orpine. But I

I'm not sure how significant Orpine is herself. Silk never met her while she was alive. But the Orpine/Mucor connection makes a lot of sense, since Orpine died because she was possessed by Mucor. In my "Dream Team" article on WolfeWiki, I try to explain many of the RttW dreams with astral projections of Mora and Mucor, etc.

There's one more short reference to the dream on p. 158 of RttW: "It was as if there were shooting stars in his hands, like the stars at the bottom of the grave to which Silk and Hyacinth had driven Orpine's body in a dream he recalled with uncanny clarity." That was on the Whorl, before Silkhorn came to Blue, so the dream he remembered must be the one in LotLS. I don't understand much of that dream, but it seems to be a prophetic, enlightened one. It told him if he went deep enough in the Whorl he would see the stars and that he must journey there: "Too deep" and "Too long." (At the time of the dream he had not seen the stars yet for himself). Covering the face of Hyacinth with the cloth used for Orpine seems to foretell that she would die before he reached Blue.

and that he must journey there: "Too deep" and "Too long."  (At the time of
the dream he had not seen the stars yet for himself). Covering the face of
Hyacinth with the cloth used for Orpine seems to foretell that she would die
before he reached Blue.

2010-08-28 Lee Berman replying to Lee Berman #

Argues that the Short Sun dream is not idle but a rational clue, and proposes a single ecological framework linking humans, Neighbors, Inhumi, Blue and Green to explain the conflicts among Mora, Hyacinth, Fava, Orpine, Chenille and Seawrack. Builds on James's idea that the trees and beasts of Green are Neighbors and Inhumi, and cites the dream's monstrous beasts and Chenille's stabbing of Orpine as evidence of the species conflict.
proposes · myth-and-structure · puzzle-solving · theories: Short Sun is an ecosystem of humans, Neighbors and Inhumi; Trees and beasts of Green are Neighbors and Inhumi; Mother and Seawrack evolved from Inhumi; Dreams in Wolfe are rational and deliberate

Well! I bumped into the dream sequence Dave partially posted last night and had the intention of starting a new thread on it but I was beaten to it! Actually I think this dream addresses another of James' theories (though to me they are all interrelated).

In addition to the stuff about Scylla and Fava and Mora and Orpine and Hyacinth (and Vadsig's hair color) this dream mentions:

First, I broadly agree with James. There is no such thing as "just a dream" in Wolfe's fiction. Unlike a real dream, product of one's irrational subconsious, Wolfean dreams are the product of a cunning, rational, very conscious intelligence. As such they can often be taken as more clear and understandable than what passes for the "real world" in a Wolfe story.

The "monstrous hairless beasts" on Green may hint at James' other theory regarding the trees and vines of Green as Neighbors and Inhumi. So for James I think this dream might be a twofer one low, low price.

For me the dream is all tied together to itself and to the other mysteries of the story. Like a certain painting in Rudesind's gallery. What seems to be a confusing collection of blobs of color will resolve itself into a recognizable picture if one will only step back far enough.

This probably isn't very profound, but for me, what I see in Short Sun, is an ecological system which involves three species, humans, Neigbors and Inhumi and two planets, Blue and Green (or perhaps Urth is distantly involved too). The participants in this ecological system are all (now) conscious and intelligent which is unusual for an ecosystem. But the participants do not fully understand their roles (least of all humans). Identity and conscious-sharing confuse the picture greatly.

There is, as in all ecosystems, competition and symbiosis between the species. Conflict and violence in the story can be a way of representing this. Why did Chenille (possessed by Mucor?) stab Orpine (whom the dream associates with Fava). I think we can step back and guess that this represents a conflict between human and inhumi.

Why is there an initial animosity followed by an uneasy truce between Babbie and Seawrack on the boat? Perhaps it represents an endless competition/conflict between Neighbors and Inhumi, with a typically befuddled human (Horn and reader) on the sidelines wondering what the hell is going on. (I do think The Mother and Seawrack are derived/evolved from Inhumi as the shapeshifting and predatory nature remains true.)

And so on for other issues in Short Sun. It works for me anyway. Just keeping a relatively simple, easy to underand GUT in mind helps me assemble the individual pieces which are so confusing and vague and nearly impossible to understand on their own.

2010-08-28 Dave Tallman replying to Lee Berman #

Argues that Orpine, Silk's prophesied murder victim, may be Blood's daughter and that Blood's plan to dump her body near Silk's manse explains the alley body in Silk's enlightenment. Responds to Lee Berman by citing Blood's plan to dispose of the body, the manse at Sun and Silver streets, and Orchid's refusal to let Blood pay for the funeral.
proposes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Orpine is Blood's daughter; Blood meant to dump Orpine's body near Silk's manse

Oh no! I'm quite certain Orpine herself is significant somehow. I

believe she is Blood's daughter (thus a connection to Mucor), but I

think there is more than that. Her murder was prophesied in Silk's first

enlightenment, but then she appears in his second enlightenment on his

wedding night.

I've never made the connection of Orpine's murder to the woman's body in the alley off Silver Street, because her body was properly buried and was never left in an alley. But it *could have been*: Blood was going to "dispose of the body somehow" until Silk intervened.

Silk's manse was at the intersection of Sun and Silver streets. Leaving the body of a murdered prostitute near Silk's home would bring shame and suspicion on the palaestra, which the vindictive Blood might well think of doing.

All right, I'll accept that theory. It's better than anything I've ever heard for that part of the enlightenment vision. Her being Blood's daughter is also plausible though not certain. Orchid was pretty vehement that Blood not pay for any part of Orpine's funeral.

2010-08-28 James Wynn replying to Dave Tallman #

Argues that Orpine is significant beyond her role as Blood's daughter linked to Mucor, pointing to her murder prophesied in Silk's first enlightenment and her appearance in his second on his wedding night. proposes · close-textual-reading · myth-and-structure · theories: Orpine is Blood's daughter linked to Mucor; Orpine and Hyacinth destined for Blue or Green

2010-08-28 James Wynn replying to Dave Tallman #

Proposes a grand unified theory that Pike's Ghost is the Rajan, that dream-travel involves time-travel, and that Hyacinth is Fava transformed, with Silk and Hyacinth retelling the Gnostic story of Simon Magus and Helena. Supports each claim with textual clues from Long Sun and Short Sun, including the flying Oreb, Hyacinth's light weight, the green umbrella, and a Calde of the Long Sun quotation.
proposes · puzzle-solving · source-and-allusion · theories: Pike's Ghost is the Rajan; Hyacinth is Fava transformed by dream-travel; Silk and Hyacinth retell Simon Magus and Helena; Long Sun and Short Sun form a single seven-volume novel

Tell you what. Now I'm feeling a little guilty. I don't want to play peek-a-boo. I'll give the synopsis without the textual proofs and give the rest later. Actually, others will probably post most them to argue the theory down.

I believe Pike's Ghost is the Rajan. I think the Rajan's presence in the grandmother's in "In Green's Jungles", the screwy time-line from Horn leaving Lizard Island until his return, and the ages of Sinew's sons on Green, is sufficient proof that dream-travel can involve Time-travel. Also, the presence of the flying Oreb when Silk's Oreb had an injured wing down stairs and asserted anyway that he had not been upstairs. Unless one credits that Pike got his own night chough in Mainframe or Heaven, it was the Rajan. I suspect he's probably also Silver Silk _and_ , maybe, he was Patera Pike. Maybe. Otherwise, we get into clone theories.

I think that after the end of "Return To the Whorl", the Rajan went back to get Fava in dreamland where he left her when her body died. Then he takes her back in Time to Viron so everything can happen as he remembers it from Silk's memory. There, Fava --who, in dream-travel looks like a teenage girl-- has some breast augmentation (as Chenille implies) and becomes Hyacinth. As you have already pointed out, favas and hyacinths are both beans. The Romans believed beans contained the souls of the dead.

Anyway, I think the Long Sun/Short Sun should properly be read as a single 7 volume novel. Asking what happened to the Rajan went they went to the Whorl is not understanding that Wolfe already told us what happened to him.

I further believe that Hyacinth's exceptional light weight -- to which the text refers TWICE-- is evidence that she is a being in dream-travel (the Rajan reported this as a symptom when he was climbing the mesa). Also, Hyacinth's promiscuity is pretty much the way inhuma behave when they get their human bodies. It's in "their reptilian nature".

I think the "taint" that Silk spoke of occurring from sex with Hyacinth refers to her actually being an inhuma -- which he learned during his wedding night enlightenment. And I think that is why Silk was so despondent on the floater. In RttW, when Jahlee comes on to Horn's son while in dream-travel, the Rajan tells him that although she is a true human woman, relations between the two of them would be wrong. I also think Hy being an inhuma is the what Silk meant when he said that little Hy defeating the guard on the floater was "one more lie".

I think Hyacinth's "father" at the Juzgada was the Rajan, fulfilling the dream of Silk's mother that Silk go to work there (Fava was the first inhumi to called him her father).
I think the belief that Hyacinth had siblings is based on a misreading of what Chenille says (it was the family she went to work for that had "a lot of sprats".
I think the mother Hy met in mainframe was the mother of the girl that FAVA's mother fed on. She was her spiritual mother. I believe the green umbrella and two gowns that Hy says her mother (this time her inhuma mother) gave her is a red herring. The green umbrella (the one she can't remember what happened to it) was the planet Green in Blue's sky. And the two gowns were her inhumi body and her dream-travel soul. I don't know if Fava has forgotten her origin (until faced with the prospect of returning to Green) or if she is covering up.

I believe the used cameo ring she bought with the picture of the older woman --because the seller said the image looked like her-- was the woman who gave her her soul (as a teenage girl). I strongly suspect that woman was Silk's birth-mother. But Orpine is on the table too.

Only quotation I'll offer here:
"The Outsider was the dancing man on a toy, and the water the polished toy-top on which he danced with Kypris, who was Hyacinth and Mother, too. _First romance_, sang the Outsider with the music box. _First romance_."
~ Calde of the Long Sun, Ch VII.

Of course, "Mother" could be another red herring. It could imply the Mother on Blue. I don't know. It gets messy there.

I believe there are at least two thematic purposes that Wolfe is riffing on with Hyacinth and Silk.
For the first, I offer this:
http://www.urth.org/whorlmap/worm.htm "The Wolfe and the Laidley Worm". -- which is something I wrote years ago when I was at the time I was debating the "Hy is a male chem" theory.

The second is that Silk/Rajan & Hyacinth/Fava is a retelling of the gnostic story of Simon Magus and Helena.
The story goes like this. Ennoia was a female half of the Cosmos. She was the first thought of Nous, whom she named "The Father". She became separated from The Father and descended through the Aeons to the Third World, eventually becoming trapped in a human body. It was she who brought the "Spirit" of the Father into the world so he could rule it. Now Simon Magus was revered The Father in the incarnation of The Son. One day, in Tyre, Simon saw a prostitute, Helena, on the roof of a brothel. He recognized her as the incarnation of Ennoia, and their marriage began the redemption of the world. The story of Helena is related in some way to the myths of Sophia.

A nice overview of Simon Magus here:
http://hermetic.com/sabazius/simon.htm
Here you will also see other little bits of the Long Sun/Short Sun story poking out like the tops of the heads of friends seen in a milling crowd.

2010-08-28 Dave Tallman replying to Lee Berman #

Disputes Dave Tallman's theory that the Rajan returned Fava's dead dream-spirit to Viron where she becomes Hyacinth, saying the time-looped astral form idea is weakly supported. Raises objections from the text: Fava's blonde hair against Hyacinth's black, dream spirits not eating, and an immortal Hyacinth undercutting Silk's grief.
disputes · puzzle-solving · close-textual-reading · theories: Fava becomes Hyacinth via time-looped astral form; Rajan returns dead Fava to Viron; Patera Pike is the Rajan with Oreb

I think that after the end of "Return To the Whorl", the Rajan went back

to get Fava in dreamland where he left her when her body died. Then he

takes her back in Time to Viron so everything can happen as he remembers

it from Silk's memory. There, Fava --who, in dream-travel looks like a

teenage girl-- has some breast augmentation (as Chenille implies) and

becomes Hyacinth. As you have already pointed out, favas and hyacinths

are both beans. The Romans believed beans contained the souls of the dead.

I agree that astral projection/dream travel can involve time travel, and that the "Patera Pike" who appeared to Silk was in fact the Rajan with his dream Oreb (now normal size with Scylla gone).

I'm less convinced about the time-looped astral form of the dead Fava becoming Hyacinth. Other than her possible light weight it seems like the evidence is mostly negative -- references to Hyacinth's parents and siblings that have to be explained away. There's an even more specific one from the Ansible Cloud Chamber 77:

26. *Who was Hyacinth's mother?* ? I don't know how to answer that. She was not a character you have known in some other connection, if that's what you mean. She was the wife of Hyacinth's father, and the mother of her sisters and brothers.

A few other objections that come to mind:

1) Fava is repeatedly mentioned as being light-haired or blonde. Hyacinth's hair is jet black.

2) Dream spirits don't eat, and derive no nourishment from eating. I think we see Hyacinth eat, though I would have to dig up the reference.

3) As an already-dead dream spirit, Hyacinth would be effectively immortal. There would be no death to distress Silk so that he would need the help of Horn's spirit to stay alive himself.

2010-08-29 António Pedro Marques replying to Lee Berman #

Argues that the names 'Seawrack' and 'Scylla' are nearly equivalent, so the misheard name could derive from the misconstrued one, and that the word's convoluted history is a deliberate reason Wolfe chose it. Builds on Lee Berman's remark about Scylla's reunion with her monster mother, and on the earlier pronunciation discussion tied to 'typhoon' etymology.
proposes · source-and-allusion · close-textual-reading · theories: Seawrack is a misheard derivation of Scylla

I don't know whether this has been pointed out before, but it is a fact that 'Seawrack' and 'Scylla' are almost equal as names go. Or, at least, the name misheard as 'Seawrack' could easily be a derivate of the name misconstrued(?) as 'Scylla'.

Yes, I know I've been implying that the greek pronunciation of a name such as Scylla would be something like ['skulla] 'SKOOL luh', but that was only in the context of knowing the earthly etymology of 'typhoon' - the briahtic etymology of the name translated as Scylla is quite another matter. And it's not that I possess some special expertise on the issue, either, I was just pointing out what I think linguists would say, because Wolfe is nothing if not rigorous in his choices. And here I must go further even than James - I think the convoluted history of the word is a *good* reason why Wolfe would use it (rather than a small nuisance he might forgive).

Whole message, with quotes · original

> little, human Scylla hoped to reunite with big mama monster Scylla.
>

2010-08-29 James Wynn replying to Dave Tallman #

Argues that Hyacinth cannot be the time-looped astral form of the dead Fava, defending her family as real rather than a sign of the Rajan's identity, and reading an ambiguous phrase about Fava's changed appearance. Responds to Dave Tallman's doubts by citing the "always seemed" passage in In Green's Jungles and the Exodus line about thighs, and speculates on Hyacinth's death and Silk's knife.
disputes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Hyacinth is not Fava's time-looped astral form; Hyacinth's mothers and siblings are real; Silk may have murdered Hyacinth

I think we should be looking for him elsewhere in the story, as well.

I believe this is a puckish answer. Hyacinth has at least two "mothers" mentioned in the story. Here Wolfe is referring to the human one.

As for her supposed siblings. It makes no sense. Not one of them were at her wedding. Not one of them is named. And after she marries the CALDE, not one of them tries to reconnect, as far as we know. Any lottery winner can tell you how peculiar that is. Heck, we even know the name of _Auk's_ brother.

I think all the answers we think Wolfe didn't address in the text, he did. But I believe this extra-textual answer about Hy's mom was worse than no answer. And Wolfe did promise to do his best to be evasive in answering these questions.

Her wig is long and light brown. _I think_ there is only one mention of her hair color is in dream-travel. If there are more, you're right, it becomes very problematic.

"And even Fava had changed only subtlety, for she had always seemed and apple-cheeked child just short of puberty, with long light brown hair and a winning smile."
~ In Green's Jungles, chap XIII

It all depends on the emphasis of the words. If you read it "for she had ALWAYS seemed" then this is the way she looked and the Rajan does not tell us specifically how she changed. If you read it "for she had always SEEMED" then he is telling us in some way how she changed.

[Hyacinth said] "I don't like not walking. My thighs are getting fat, I can feel it."
Maytera Marble overtook them. "They can't be, dear, you don't eat anything. I'm worried about you."
Exodus, chap XVI

I'm not sure that dream-travellers CAN'T eat. Only that they don't need to. And, anyway, as an inhuma, Hy would be quite good at pretending to eat (just as the Rajan says he is). But Marble is not fooled it seems.

Well, Hy didn't die of old age. As Roy pointed recently, it had only been some 20 years after her marriage.
Dream-travellers CAN die, however. They can be killed and buried like anyone else. When the traveller returns however, he is mute and amnesiac. His soul has been lost. But Hy-as-Fava would have no body to return to. How did Hyacinth die? Once again, Orpine lurks in the back of my mind. Not that Hy was murdered in Viron, as far as I know. But Silk has a knife at her wake--and based on the "scratches" on his wrist-- he killed himself with it. That Silk himself murdered her is on the table considering the Rajan's murder of Jahlee (I know how revolting that thought is, and I only offer it as an example of how little we know).

2010-08-29 Dave Tallman replying to Lee Berman #

Argues against the theory that the Rajan is Patera Pike and that Hyacinth had no living relatives, saying astral travel need not span years and a brief glimpse of an older face suffices. Objects that the theory makes the Rajan morally repugnant by forcing Hyacinth into years of degradation to recreate his lost love, citing her bitterness toward men in the text.
disputes · close-textual-reading · puzzle-solving · theories: Patera Pike is the Rajan's older self; Typhon clone theory of Pike's face; Rajan forced Hyacinth into degradation to recreate his love

Yes, there may be other places, but given the way astral travel is portrayed I don't look for years-long roles (like the entire lifetime of Patera Pike during the time Silk knew him). It's not necessary for the known sighting -- Silk only got a glimpse of the face of his older self, shadowed by the culotte. If you insist on identical faces, then the Typhon clone theory can be brought in, but I don't think it's required.

I'm not sure about the argument from silence for the lack of relatives for Hyacinth. She was probably estranged from them all, so they wouldn't be invited to her wedding. If some of them tried to reconnect and were rebuffed that might not be important enough to include in the story. She was bitter about her past: "Hyacinth had hated all men, had hated men in the aggregate, because of things that had been said to her and things she had been forced to do for money, humiliations worse than spoiled fish."

That seems to me the worst thing about this theory -- what it does to the Rajan's character. I could forgive Silk for killing Hyacinth in a fit of rage and despair, but to force a young woman into years of degradation merely to recreate the woman he loved is so morally repugnant that it would destroy the story for me. He's better than that.
